## 6

Advanced Topics in Predictive Modeling

Predictive analytics is still as much art as it is science. For a given business problem and a set of real-world data, developing predictive analytics and machine learning models that are capable of producing highly accurate and unbiased predictions that are interpretable and explainable by humans while computationally efficient and easily deployable is a challenging task for any data scientist. Although we have seen significant improvements in the standardization and automation of methodologies (such as the CRISP methodology described in [Chapter 3](#ch03.xhtml#ch03), “[Standardized Processes for Predictive Analytics](#ch03.xhtml#ch03)”) and creation of best/better practices for model building in recent years, the true optimization of the complete data science/business analytics process still remains as a utopia. Many, including the author of this book, believe that at best we are halfway to achieving this endeavor. In this chapter, we cover some of the relatively novel concepts and approaches proposed to pave the road toward achieving the utopia. Specifically, this chapter covers ensemble modeling, the bias–variance trade-off, how to handle imbalanced data problems, and the human interpretability of machine learning models.

### **Model Ensembles**

Ensembles (more appropriately called model ensembles or ensemble modeling) are relatively new tools in data science and predictive modeling. With ensemble modeling, outcomes produced by two or more analytics models are combined into a compound output. Ensembles are primarily used for prediction modeling, where the scores of two or more models are combined to produce better predictions. The predictions can be either classification-type predictions (predicting a class label) or regression-/estimation-type predictions (estimating a numeric output variable).

Although use of ensembles has been dominated by prediction-type modeling, it can also be used for other analytics tasks, such as clustering and association rule mining. That is, model ensembles can be used for supervised as well as unsupervised machine learning tasks. Traditionally, these machine learning procedures focused on identifying and building the best possible model (often the most accurate predictor on the holdout data) from a large number of alternative model types. Analysts and scientists used an elaborate experimental process that mainly relied on trial and error to improve each single model’s performance (defined by some predetermined metrics, such as, prediction accuracy) to its best possible level so that the best of the models could be used/deployed for the task at hand. The ensemble approach turns this thinking around. Rather than build models and select the single best model to use/deploy, the ensemble approach builds many models and uses them all for the task they are intended to perform (e.g., prediction).

#### ***Motivation: Why Do We Need Model Ensembles?***

Usually researchers and practitioners build ensembles for two main reasons: better accuracy and more stable/robust/consistent/reliable outcomes. Numerous research studies and publications over the past two decades have shown that ensembles almost always improve predictive accuracy for the given problem and rarely predict more poorly than the single models (Abbott, 2014).

Ensembles began to appear in the data mining/analytics literature in the 1990s, motivated by the limited success obtained by the earlier works on combining forecasts. By the early to mid-2000s, ensembles became popular and almost essential to winning data mining and predictive modeling competitions. One of the most popular examples of ensembles winning competitions is perhaps the famous Netflix case of an open competition that solicited researchers and practitioners to predict user ratings of films based on historical ratings. Netflix offered a prize of $1 million for the team that could reduce the root means squared error (RMSE) of the then-existing Netflix internal prediction algorithm by the largest margin but no less than 10 percentage points. The winner, runner-up, and nearly all the teams at the top of the leaderboard used model ensembles in their submissions. The winning submission was the result of an ensemble containing hundreds of predictive models.

When it comes to justifying the use of ensembles, Vorhies (2016) puts it the best: If you want to win a predictive analytics competition or at least get a respectable place on the leaderboard, you have to embrace and intelligently use model ensembles. Kaggle has become the premier platform for data scientists to showcase their talents. According to Vorhies, the Kaggle competitions are like Formula 1 racing for data science. Winners edge out competitors at the fourth decimal place, and their solutions are as distinct from the competitors’ solutions as Formula 1 race cars are from the cars most of us drive. The amount of time devoted and the extreme techniques used would not always be appropriate for an ordinary data science production project, but like paddle shifters and exotic suspensions, some of those improvements and advanced features find their way into the day-to-day life and practice of analytics professionals.

In addition to Kaggle competitions, reputable associations such as the Association for Computing Machinery’s (ACM’s) Special Interest Group (SIG) on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining (SIGKDD) and Pacific-Asia Conference in Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining (PAKDD) regularly organize competition (often called “cups”) for the community of data scientists to demonstrate their competence—sometimes for monetary rewards but most often simply for bragging rights. Some of the popular analytics companies, such as SAS Institute and Teradata Corporation, organize similar competitions for (and extend a variety of relatively modest awards to) both graduate and undergraduate students in universities all over the world, usually in concert with their regular analytics conferences.

It is not just their accuracy that makes model ensembles popular and unavoidably necessary. Yes, it has been shown time and time again that ensembles can improve model accuracy, but they can also improve model robustness, stability, and reliability. These advantages of model ensembles are perhaps even more important than accuracy in situations where reliable prediction is of the essence. A model ensemble combines multiple models into a single prediction outcome (using some form of averaging) so that no single model dominates the final predicted value of the models; this reduces the likelihood of making a way-off-target prediction. [Figure 6.1](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig01) graphically illustrates a model ensemble for classification-type prediction problems. Although some varieties exist, most ensemble modeling methods follow this general process. From left to right, [Figure 6.1](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig01) illustrates the general tasks of data acquisition and data preparation, followed by cross-validation and model building and testing, and, finally assembling/combining the individual model outcomes and assessing the resultant predictions.

![Figure 6.1 A Graphical Depiction of a Model Ensemble for Prediction Modeling](graphics/06fig01.jpg)

**Figure 6.1** A Graphical Depiction of a Model Ensemble for Prediction Modeling

Another way to look at ensembles is from the perspective of collective wisdom or crowdsourcing. In the popular book *The Wisdom of Crowds* (Surowiecki, 2005), the author proposes that a better decision can be made through crowdsourcing, where many (even uninformed) opinions are aggregated into a decision that is superior to the best expert’s opinion. In his book, Surowiecki describes four characteristics necessary for the group opinion to work well and not degenerate into the opposite effect of the poor decisions sometimes made based on the “madness of crowds”: diversity of opinion, independence, decentralization, and aggregation. The first three of these characteristics relate to how the individual decisions are made: Group members must have information different from what others in the group have and must not be affected by the others in the group. The last characteristic merely states that the decisions must be combined. These four principles/characteristics lay a foundation for building better model ensembles as well. Each predictive model has a voice in the final decision. The diversity of opinion can be measured by the correlation of the predictive values themselves; if all of the predictions are highly correlated with one another—that is, if the models nearly all agree—there is no foreseeable advantage in combining them. The decentralization characteristic can be achieved by resampling data or through case weights, where each model uses either different records from a common data set or at least uses the records with weights that are different from the other models (Abbott, 2014).

One of the prevalent concepts in statistics and predictive modeling that is highly relevant to model ensembles is the bias–variance trade-off. Before delving into the different types of model ensembles, it is necessary to review and understand the bias–variance trade-off principle (as it applies to the field of statistics or machine learning). In predictive analytics, *bias* refers to the error, and *variance* refers to the consistency (more accurately, the lack of consistency) in predictive accuracy of models applied to other data sets. The best models are expected to have low bias (i.e., low error or high accuracy) and low variance (i.e., consistency of accuracy from data set to data set). Unfortunately, there is always a trade-off between these two metrics in building predictive models so that improving one results in worsening the other. You can achieve low bias on training data, but the model may suffer from high variance on holdout/validation data because the models may have been overtrained/overfit. For instance, the *k*\-NN algorithm with *k* = 1 is an example of a low-bias model, which is perfect for a training data set, but it is susceptible to high variance on a test/validation data set. Use of cross-validation along with proper model ensembles seems to be the current best practice in handling the trade-off between bias and variance in predictive modeling.

#### ***Types of Model Ensembles***

Using ensembles, or teams of predictive models working together, has been the fundamental strategy for developing accurate and robust analytics models. Although ensembles have been around for quite a while, their popularity and effectiveness have surfaced in a significant way only within the past decade, as they have continually improved in parallel with the rapidly improving software and hardware capabilities. *Model ensemble* makes many people think of decision tree ensembles such as random forest and boosted trees. However, generally speaking, model ensembles can be classified into four groups in two dimensions, as shown in [Figure 6.2](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig02). The first dimension is the method type (the *x*\-axis in [Figure 6.2](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig02)), where the ensembles can be grouped into bagging or boosting types. The second dimension is the model type (the *y*\-axis in [Figure 6.2](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig02)), where the ensembles can be grouped into homogeneous or heterogeneous types (Abbott, 2014).

![Figure 6.2 A Simple Taxonomy for Model Ensembles](graphics/06fig02.jpg)

**Figure 6.2** A Simple Taxonomy for Model Ensembles

As their name implies, *homogeneous* ensembles combine the outcomes of two or more models of the same type, such as decision trees. In fact, a vast majority of homogeneous ensembles are developed using a combination of decision tree structures. The two most common categories of homogeneous ensembles that use decision trees are bagging and boosting (described in detail later in this chapter). *Heterogeneous* ensembles, as you would guess, combine the outcomes of two or more different types of models, such as decision trees, artificial neural networks, logistic regression, and support vector machines. One of the key success factors in ensemble modeling is to use models that are fundamentally different from one another, that look at the data from different perspectives; this is similar to the idea of crowdsourcing. Because of the way they combine the outcomes of different model types, heterogeneous ensembles are also called information fusion models (Delen & Sharda, 2010) or stacking models (both discussed later in this chapter).

#### ***Bagging***

Bagging is the simplest and most common ensemble method. Leo Breiman, a very well-respected scholar in the world of statistics and analytics, first published a description of the bagging (short for bootstrap aggregating) algorithm at Berkeley in 1996 (Breiman, 1996). The idea behind begging is quite simple yet powerful: Build multiple decision trees from resampled data and combine the predicted values through averaging or voting. The resampling method Breiman used was bootstrap sampling (i.e., sampling with replacement), which creates replicates of some records in the training data. With this selection method, on average, about 37% of the records will not be included at all in the training data set (Abbott, 2014).

Although bagging was first developed for decision trees, the idea can be applied to any predictive modeling algorithm that produces outcomes with sufficient variation in the predicted values. Although rare in practice, the other predictive modeling algorithms that are potential candidates for bagging-type model ensembles include neural networks, Naive Bayes, *k*\-nearest neighbor (for low values of *k*), and, to a lesser degree, logistic regression. *k*\-nearest neighbor is not a good candidate for bagging if the value of *k* is already large; the algorithm votes or averages predictions, and with larger values of *k*, predictions are already very stable and have low variance.

Bagging can be used for both classification and regression/estimation prediction problems. In classification-type prediction problems, all of the participant models’ outcomes (class assignments) are combined by using either a simple or complex/weighted majority voting mechanism. The class label that gets the most/highest votes becomes the aggregated/ensemble prediction for that sample/record. In a regression-/estimation-type prediction problem where the output/target variable is a number, all of the participant models’ outcomes (numeric estimations) are combined using either a simple or complex/weighted averaging mechanism. [Figure 6.3](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig03) provides a graphical depiction of a decision tree bagging algorithm.

![Figure 6.3 Bagging Decision Tree Ensembles](graphics/06fig03.jpg)

**Figure 6.3** Bagging Decision Tree Ensembles

One of the key questions in bagging is “How many bootstrap samples, also called replicates, should be created?” According to Breiman, “my sense of it is that fewer are required when *y* \[dependent variable\] is numerical and more are required with an increasing number of classes \[for classification-type prediction problems\].” Breiman typically used 10 to 25 bootstrap replicates and found significant improvements with as few as 10 replicates. Overfitting the models is an important requirement for building good bagged ensembles. Overfitting each model leads to low bias, but the decision tree generally ends up with worse accuracy on held-out data. However, bagging is a variance-reduction technique; the averaging of predictions smooths the predictions so that they behave in a more stable (i.e., less-variant) way on new data.

As mentioned earlier, the diversity of model predictions is a key factor in creating effective ensembles. One way to measure the diversity of predictions is to examine the correlation of predicted values. If the correlations between model predictions are always very high (i.e., greater than 0.95), there is little additional predictive information each model brings to the ensemble, and therefore little improvement in accuracy is achievable. Generally, it is best to have correlations less than 0.9 at most. The correlations should be computed from the model propensities or predicted probabilities rather than from the {0,1} classification value. Bootstrap sampling in bagging is the key to introducing diversity in the models. You can think of the bootstrap sampling methodology as creating case weights for each record; some records are included multiple times in the training data (i.e., their weights are 1, 2, 3, or more), and others are not included at all (i.e., their weights are equal to 0) (Abbott, 2014).

#### ***Boosting***

Boosting is another ensemble method, and it is perhaps the most common method after bagging. Yoav Freund and Robert E. Schapire first introduced the boosting algorithm in separate publications in the early 1990s, and then, in a 1996 joint publication (Yoav & Schapire, 1996), they introduced the well-known boosting algorithm AdaBoost. The idea behind boosting is quite straightforward. First, you build a rather simple classification model; the model only needs to be slightly better than random chance, so for a binary classification problem, it only needs to be slightly better than a 50% correct classification. In the first pass, the records used in the algorithm have equal case weights, as would normally be done in a predictive model. The errors in the predicted values for each case are noted. The correctly classified records/cases/samples have their case weights stay the same, or perhaps reduced, and the records that are incorrectly classified have their case weights increased. Then a second simple model is built on these weighted cases (i.e., the transformed/weighted training data set). In other words, for the second model, records that were incorrectly classified are “boosted” through case weights to be considered more strongly or seriously in the construction of the new prediction model. In each iteration, the records that are incorrectly predicted (i.e., the ones that are difficult to classify) get their case weights increased so that the algorithm pays more attention to these records until, hopefully, they are finally classified correctly.

This process of boosting is often repeated tens or even hundreds of times. After the tens or hundreds of iterations, the final predictions are made based on a weighted average of the predictions from all the models. [Figure 6.4](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig04) illustrates the simple process of boosting in building decision tree–type model ensembles. As shown, each tree takes the most current data set (i.e., the one with the most recently boosted case weights) to build another tree. The feedback from incorrectly predicted cases is used as an indicator to determine which cases to boost and to what extent (in direction and magnitude) for the training samples/cases.

![Figure 6.4 Boosting-Type Ensembles for Decision Trees](graphics/06fig04.jpg)

**Figure 6.4** Boosting-Type Ensembles for Decision Trees

Although they look quite similar in structure and purpose, bagging and boosting employ slightly different strategies to utilize the training data set and to achieve the goal of building the best possible prediction ensemble. There are two key differences between bagging and boosting. First, bagging uses a bootstrap sample of the cases to build decision trees, whereas boosting uses the complete training data set. Second, whereas bagging creates independent, simple trees for the ensemble, boosting creates dependent trees (with each tree “learning” from the previous one to pay more attention to the incorrectly predicted cases) that collectively contribute to the final ensemble.

Boosting methods are designed to work with weak learners (i.e., simple models); the component models in a boosted ensemble are simple models with high bias and low variance. The improvement with boosting is better, as with bagging, when algorithms that are unstable predictors are used. Decision trees are most often used in boosted models. Naive Bayes is also used with boosting but produced rather marginal improvements over a single model. Empirically speaking, boosting typically produces better model accuracy than single decision trees and bagging-type ensembles.

#### ***Variants of Bagging and Boosting***

Bagging and boosting were the first ensemble methods that appeared in predictive analytics software (primarily with decision tree algorithms). Since their introduction, many other approaches to building ensembles have been developed and made available, particularly in open source software (both as part of open analytics platforms like KNIME and Orange and as class libraries in R and Python). The most popular and successful (advanced) variants of bagging and boosting are random forest and stochastic gradient boosting, respectively.

##### ***Random Forests***

Breiman (2000) first introduced random forest (RF) as a modification to the simple bagging algorithm. As with bagging, the RF algorithm begins with bootstrap sampled data, and one decision tree is built from each bootstrap sample. Compared to simple bagging, there is, however, an important twist to the random forest algorithm: At each split in the tree, starting from the very first split, rather than considering all input variables as candidates, only a random subset of variables is considered. Hence, in random forest, the bootstrap sampling technique applies to both random selection of cases and random selection of features (i.e., input variables).

The number of cases and the number of variables to consider, along with how many trees to construct are parameters to consider in building random forest models. Typically, the default number of variables to consider as candidates at each split point is the square root of the total number of candidate inputs. For example, if there are 100 candidate inputs for the model, a random 10 inputs are candidates for each split. This means that it is unlikely that the same inputs will be available for splits at parent and child nodes in a given tree, so the tree will be forced to find alternative ways to maximize the accuracy of subsequent splits. Therefore, there is an intentionally created two-fold diversity mechanism built into the tree construction process: random selection of cases and variables. Random forest models produce prediction outcomes that are usually more accurate than simple bagging and are often more accurate than simple boosting (i.e., AdaBoost).

##### ***Stochastic Gradient Boosting***

AdaBoost is only one of many boosting algorithms currently documented in the literature. In commercial software, AdaBoost is still the most commonly used boosting technique, but dozens of boosting variants can be found in open source software packages. One interesting boosting algorithm that has recently gained popularity due to its superior performance is the stochastic gradient boosting (SGB) algorithm created by Jerry Friedman at Stanford University. Friedman (2001) also developed an advanced version of the AdaBoost algorithm that he called multiple additive regression trees (MART), which was later branded as TreeNet by Salford Systems in its software tool. Like other boosting algorithms, the MART algorithm builds successive, simple trees and combines them additively. Typically, the simple trees are more than stumps; each tree usually contains up to six terminal nodes. After building the first tree, errors (also called *residuals*) are computed. The second tree and all subsequent trees then use residuals as the target variable. Subsequent trees identify patterns that relate inputs to small and large errors. Poor prediction of the errors results in large errors to predict in the next tree, and good predictions of the errors result in small errors to predict in the next tree. Typically, hundreds of trees are built, and the final predictions are an additive combination of the predictions that is, interestingly, a piecewise constant model because each tree is itself a piecewise constant model. However, hundreds of trees are typically included in the ensemble (Abbott, 2014). The TreeNet algorithm has won multiple data modeling competitions since its introduction and has proven to be an accurate predictor; in addition, very little data cleaning is needed for the trees before modeling.

##### ***Stacking***

Stacking (a.k.a. stacked generalization or super learner) is a heterogeneous ensemble method. Some analytics professionals believe it is the best ensemble technique, but it is also the least understood (and the most difficult to explain). Due to its two-step model training procedure, stacking can seem to be an overly complicated form of ensemble modeling. Basically, stacking creates an ensemble from a diverse group of strong learners. In the process, it interjects a metadata step called a “super learner” or “meta learner.” The intermediate meta-classifiers forecast how accurately the primary classifiers have been and are used as the basis for adjustments and corrections (Vorhies, 2016). The process of stacking is figuratively illustrated in [Figure 6.5](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig05).

![Figure 6.5 Stacking Type Model Ensembles](graphics/06fig05.jpg)

**Figure 6.5** Stacking Type Model Ensembles

As shown in [Figure 6.5](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig05), in constructing a stacking type model ensemble, a number of diverse strong classifiers are first trained using bootstrapped samples of the training data, creating Tier 1 classifiers (each optimized to its full potential for the best possible prediction outcomes). The outputs of the Tier 1 classifiers are then used to train a Tier 2 classifier (i.e., a meta-classifier) (Wolpert, 1992). The idea is to determine whether training data have been properly learned. For example, if a particular classifier incorrectly learned a certain region of the feature space, and it consistently misclassifies instances coming from that region, then the Tier 2 classifier may be able to learn this behavior, and along with the learned behaviors of other classifiers, it can correct such improper training. Cross-validation selection is typically used for training the Tier 1 classifiers: The entire training data set is divided into *k* mutually exclusive subsets, and each Tier 1 classifier is trained on (a different set of) *k* – 1 subsets of the training data. Each classifier is then evaluated on the *k*th subset, which was not seen during training. The outputs of these classifiers on their pseudo-training blocks, along with the actual correct labels for those blocks, constitute the training data set for the Tier 2 classifier.

##### ***Information Fusion***

As part of heterogeneous model ensembles, information fusion combines (fuses) the output (i.e., predictions) of different types of models, such as decision trees, artificial neural networks, logistic regression, support vector machines, Naive Bayes, *k*\-nearest neighbor, and their variants, among other. There is a difference between stacking and information fusion in that information fusion has no “meta-modeling” or “super-learners.” It simply combines the outcomes of heterogeneous strong classifiers using simple or weighted voting (for classification) or simple or weighted averaging (for regression). Therefore, it is simpler and less computationally demanding than stacking. In the process of combining the outcomes of multiple models, either simple voting (where each model contributes one vote) or a weighted combination of voting (where each model contributes based on its prediction accuracy, so that more accurate models have higher weight values) can be used. Regardless of the combination method, heterogeneous ensembles have been shown to be invaluable additions to data mining and predictive modeling projects. [Figure 6.6](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig06) graphically illustrates the process of building information fusion model ensembles.

![Figure 6.6 An Illustration of the Building Process for Information Fusion Model Ensembles](graphics/06fig06.jpg)

**Figure 6.6** An Illustration of the Building Process for Information Fusion Model Ensembles

#### ***Ensembles Are Not Perfect!***

When you are asked to build a prediction model (or any other analytics model for that matter), you are expected to develop some of the popular model ensembles along with the standard individual models. Ensembles are often more accurate and almost always more robust and reliable than individual models. Although they seem like silver bullets, model ensembles are not without shortcomings. Their most common and important shortcomings are complexity and lack of transparency.

##### ***Complexity***

Model ensembles are more complex than individual models. The core data science principle Occam’s razor says that simpler models are more likely to generalize better, so it is better to reduce/regularize complexity or, in other words, to simplify the models so that the inclusion of each term, coefficient, or split in a model is justified by its power of reducing the error a sufficient amount. One way to quantify the relationship between accuracy and complexity is taken from information theory in the form of information theoretic criteria, such as the Akaike information criterion (AIC), the Bayesian information criterion (BIC), and minimum description length (MDL). Data scientists have borrowed these criteria from statisticians and are using them for variable selection in predictive modeling. Information theoretic criteria require a reduction in model error to justify additional model complexity. Do model ensembles violate Occam’s razor? Ensembles, after all, are much more complex than single models. According to Abbott (2014), if the accuracy of an ensemble is better on held-out data than the accuracy of a single model, then the answer is no—as long as we think of model complexity in terms of not just computational complexity but behavioral complexity. Therefore, we should not fear that adding computational complexity (more terms, splits, or weights) will necessarily increase the complexity of models because sometimes an ensemble significantly reduces the behavioral complexity.

##### ***Transparency***

The interpretation of ensembles can become quite difficult. If you build a random forest ensemble containing 200 trees, how do you describe why a prediction has a particular value? You can examine each of the trees individually, though doing so is clearly not practical. For this reason, ensembles are often considered black-box models, meaning that what they do is not transparent to the modeler or domain expert. However, you can look at the split statistics (which variables are more often picked to split early in those 200 trees) to artificially judge the level of contribution (a pseudo variable’s importance) each variable makes to the trained model ensemble. Compared to a single decision tree, this is still difficult, and it is not a very intuitive way to interpret how a model performs. Another way to determine which inputs to the model are most important is to perform a sensitivity analysis.

In addition to the complexity and transparency issues, model ensembles can also be difficult and computationally expensive to build and deploy. [Table 6.1](#ch06.xhtml#ch06tab01) shows the pros and cons of model ensembles as they compared to individual models.

**Table 6.1** Advantages and Shortcomings of Model Ensembles

 
| **Advantages** || **Description** |
| --- | --- |
| Accuracy || Model ensembles usually provide more accuracy than do individuals models. |
| --- | --- |
| Robustness || Model ensembles tend to be more robust against outliers and noise in the data than are individual models. |
| Reliability (stable) || Because of the variance reduction, model ensembles tend to produce more stable, reliable, and believable results than do individual models. |
| Coverage || Model ensembles tend to have better coverage of the hidden complex patterns in the data sets than do individual models. |
| **Shortcomings** || **Description** |
| Complexity || Model ensembles are much more complex than individual models. |
| Computationally expensive || Compared to building individual models, building ensembles requires more time and computational power. |
| Lack of transparency (explainability) || Because of their complexity, it is harder to understand the inner structure of model ensembles (how they do what they do) than it is to understand the structure of individual models. |
| Harder to deploy || Model ensembles are much harder to deploy in an analytics-based managerial decision system than are individual models. |

In summary, model ensembles are the new frontier for predictive modelers who are interested in accuracy as they either reduce errors in the models or reduce the risk that the models will behave erratically. Evidence for this is clear from the dominance of ensembles in predictive analytics and data mining competitions: ensembles always win.

The good news for predictive modelers is that many techniques for building ensembles are built into software already. The most popular ensemble algorithms (i.e., bagging, boosting, and stacking and their variants) are available in nearly every commercial and open source software tool. Building customized ensembles is also supported in many software products, whether based on a single algorithm or a heterogeneous ensemble.

Ensembles aren’t appropriate for every solution; the applicability of ensembles is determined by the modeling objectives defined during business understanding and problem definition. However, ensembles should be part of every predictive modeler’s and data scientist’s modeling arsenal.

### **Bias–Variance Trade-off in Predictive Analytics**

In developing machine learning models for predictive analytics, there is always a trade-off between simplicity/parsimony and complexity, generalizability (i.e., capture of the signal) and overfitting (including the noise), predictive accuracy and explainability, overtraining/curve-fitting and undertraining, training error and validation error. The bias–variance is a rather common concept in statistics and machine learning that generally deals with the assessment of a trained predictive model’s usefulness and accuracy. It is considered a crucial issue in predictive modeling, data mining, machine learning, and supervised learning. Although the best model is achieved when both bias and variance are minimized, there is, as the word *trade-off* in the name implies, a balancing act. The concepts bias and variance oppose each other; they do not go in the same direction, and improving one worsens the other. Finding the optimal combination of bias and variance to achieve the best predictive model for a given problem and data combination is anything but a trivial process; it often requires consideration of best (or better) practices along with extensive experimentation and comparative analyses; even then, the obtained model cannot be confirmed as globally “optimal.”

#### ***What Are Bias and Variance?***

*Bias* is the difference between a model’s prediction output and the ground truth (i.e., the real/actual values) for a given prediction problem. It is the training error. A model with high bias captures very little of the desired signal (i.e., patterns and relationships) in the training data and therefore produces an oversimplified/underfit model. As a result, such a model leads to high error values on both training and testing data.

*Variance*, on the other hand, is a testing error. It is the variability of a model’s predictions for a given data set that characterizes the robustness of the prediction model. A model with high variance is the one that captures not only the signal (i.e., generalized patterns and relationships) in the data but also the noise and therefore produces an overly complex/overfit model. As a result, such a model performs well on training data but does very poorly on test data.

To graphically and intuitively illustrate the trade-off between bias and variance, we often use bullseye diagrams like the ones shown in [Figure 6.7](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig07). Bias values are depicted on the *y*\-axis, and variance values are depicted on the *x*\-axis. For simplicity, [Figure 6.7](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig07) is split into four quadrants (Q1 through Q4) in a two-dimensional Cartesian coordinate system along the dichotomized values of the *x*\- and *y*\-axes to show low and high. The simple interpretations of these quadrants are as follows:

*   **Q1: High bias, low variance.** The predictions are consistent but are off-target/inaccurate (representing a consistently underperforming prediction model).
    
*   **Q2: High bias, high variance.** The predictions are neither consistent nor accurate (representing an underperforming and inconsistent prediction model).
    
*   **Q3: Low bias, low variance.** The predictions are both consistent and accurate (representing the ideal prediction model).
    
*   **Q4: Low bias, high variance.** The predictions are inconsistent but somewhat accurate (representing a reasonably well-performing but inconsistent prediction model).
    

![Figure 6.7 A Bullseye Diagram Illustration of the Bias–Variance Trade-off](graphics/06fig07.jpg)

**Figure 6.7** A Bullseye Diagram Illustration of the Bias–Variance Trade-off

#### ***Overfitting Versus Underfitting***

The process of training a predictive model is often characterized as “fitting” (e.g., fitting a model to a given data set). [Figure 6.8](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig08) shows the three levels of fitting (also called training or learning)—overfitting, underfitting, and balanced fitting—in a two-dimensional feature space. Overfitting (also called overtraining) is the notion of making the model too specific to the training data), and by doing so, capturing not only the signal but also the noise in the data set. As a result, the overtrained model (especially in the case of highly parametrized model types, such as neural networks) perform exceptionally well (at the “too good to be true” level) on the training data and do very poorly on the test data. This phenomenon is also commonly characterized on the bias–variance trade-off continuum as low-bias/high-variance outcome.

![Figure 6.8 The Three Levels of Fitting a Model to the Training Data](graphics/06fig08.jpg)

**Figure 6.8** The Three Levels of Fitting a Model to the Training Data

On the other hand, underfitting (also called undertraining) is the notion of leaving the model too generic by not completing the training (i.e., the learning process) from the training data (i.e., not capturing the signal inherent into the training data) and, therefore, not performing as well as expected on the training data (or the testing data). (Although an undertrained model may perform equally well on the test data because of its poor performance on the training data, such performance may not be nearly sufficient for any practical purposes.) Underfitting is commonly characterized on the bias–variance trade-off continuum as high-bias/low-variance outcome.

What is desired for a predictive model is to avoid both overtraining and undertraining and, by doing so, to achieve an optimal balance in fitting the model to the available training data.

#### ***How to Determine the Optimal Level of Training***

Most machine learning tools are formulated to learn from data in an iterative manner. With each iteration, a machine learning tool tries to incorporate more and more details deduced/inferred from the data in order to minimize the error. Since predictive models are at the core of supervised learning—part of the machine learning family of methods—during training/learning, they aim to minimize the error (difference or delta) between the model output and the actual output. Each iteration focuses on reducing this error by adjusting the model parameters. For instance, in artificial neural networks, the learning process aims to minimize the difference between model output (the value at the output layer) and the actual output of the data. Similarly, in decision tree induction, the learning process keeps growing the tree with new branches in order to purify the outputs at the leaf nodes (to achieve the perfect accuracy or matching).

[Figure 6.9](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig09) graphically illustrates the change in the prediction error (and its reflection in the bias and variance curves/values) on the *y*\-axis as the complexity of the model (and the training/learning process) progressively changes/evolves. As shown in the figure, at the left end of the *x*\-axis, the simplest, least complex models—the undertrained, underlearned, underfitted models—have large error values (i.e., low prediction accuracy on the training), a high level of bias, and a low level of variance. The most complex models, at the right end of the *x*\-axis—the overtrained, overlearned, overfitted models—have very low error values (i.e., low prediction accuracy on the training data), a low level of bias, and a high level of variance.

![Figure 6.9 The Nonlinear Relationship Between Error/Complexity and Bias/Variance](graphics/06fig09.jpg)

**Figure 6.9** The Nonlinear Relationship Between Error/Complexity and Bias/Variance

Building a good prediction model requires finding an optimal balance between the errors related to bias and variance. The optimal balance between the bias and variance can be determined by summing the error values that represent the two curves into a total error curve (with a concave shape) and then identifying/calculating the lowest point in the curve. This lowest point can be used to find the corresponding “optimal” values in terms of complexity and the corresponding best achievable error values. The individual errors and the total error associated with each of the three curves in [Figure 6.9](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig09) can be formulated mathematically as follows:

![Images](graphics/pg214-01.jpg)

![Images](graphics/pg214-02.jpg)

*Error*(*x*) is the sum of the three error terms: Bias², Variance, and Noise (i.e., the irreducible error). Irreducible error, as the name implies, cannot be reduced further through more training and use of more sophistication in the model. It is considered the noise in the model that you have to live with, and it is usually due to missing (unrepresented) information in the variable space. In real-world problems, which are complex and need to be simplified via modeling assumptions, there will always be some degree of missing information (input variables), and hence, a model will never be perfectly or completely able to produce zero noise.

### **Imbalanced Data Problems in Predictive Analytics**

Developing prediction models using imbalanced data sets is one of the most critical challenges in the field of data mining and decision analytics. A data set is *imbalanced* when the distribution of different classes in the target variable are significantly dissimilar. For instance, in the case of binary classification problems in medical testing, the data may have many more examples that belong to one class (e.g., negative examples) compared to the other class (e.g., positive examples). Usually, we call the class with fewer examples the minority class and the class with more examples the majority class. Although most of the discussion on imbalanced data problems centers around the binary classification problems, the phenomenon also applies to multiclass classification problems as well as to problems that deal with numeric target variables (i.e., regression- or estimation-type prediction problems). Decision modeling based on imbalanced data sets is quite common in real-world problems, including medical diagnosis, fraud detection, churn analysis, email spam identification, outlier detection, bankruptcy prediction, and network intrusion detection.

The issues related to imbalanced data problems are especially obvious in the development of clinical decision support systems where the cases with a rare disease would usually constitute a very small proportion of the total patient population. For instance, a study by Piri et al. (2017) involved the development of a clinical decision support system for diagnosing diabetic retinopathy patients using only a small set of diabetes test results and patient demographic information (contained within the EHR database). In this data set—a population of several hundred thousand diabetic patients—only about 5% of the patients had diabetic retinopathy, and the remaining 95% did not have the disease.

A less severely imbalanced data problem occurred in a decision support system developed for educational institutions (Thammasiri et al., 2014). The main goals of this educational decision support system were to identify, explain, and mitigate freshmen student attrition at a higher education institution. This student attrition prediction problem involved a data set that contained about four times more negative samples (freshmen students who did not drop out) than positive samples (freshmen students who did drop out). [Figure 6.10](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig10) graphically illustrates the data imbalance situation dealt with while developing this system. As shown on the right side of the figure (the “model assessment” pane), the imbalanced data produced very high predictive accuracy on the majority class but performed very poorly on the minority class; balanced data produced significantly better prediction accuracy for both minority and majority classes. Although the overall accuracy achieved with an imbalanced data set seems to be scientifically higher, this is a rather deceptive perception and is often characterized as “fool’s gold” in data mining literature. Keep in mind that a balanced data set allows machine learning techniques to produce reasonably high prediction accuracy for both classes.

![Figure 6.10 A Graphical Depiction of an Imbalanced Data Problem](graphics/06fig10.jpg)

**Figure 6.10** A Graphical Depiction of an Imbalanced Data Problem

But why does an imbalanced data set lead to poor prediction outcomes? Machine learning methods learn from the training data in a manner somewhat similar to the way that humans learn from their experiences. That is, in humans (and perhaps in most animals as well), memory and recall are largely determined by repeated events and experiences. Frequently observed experiences lead to more permanent and vivid memories and lead to easier and more accurate recall and recognition. In contrast, experiences that are observed less frequently are often overlooked or simply ignored, and hence they are often not accurately recalled when needed. In machine learning, a similar learning process takes place, where the method produces biased predictive models that focus on the majority class patterns while conveniently ignoring the minority class specifics.

#### ***How to Solve Imbalanced Data Problems***

There are various ways to mitigate imbalanced data problems and thereby improve the performance of a given predictive model. [Figure 6.11](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig11) shows a simple taxonomy of the methods used to handle imbalanced data problems. Although these methods may differ in level of algorithmic sophistication, the goal of all of these methods is to make sure the prediction method (or the underlying algorithm) pays nearly equal/fair attention to patterns presented by all classes—majority, minority, and anything in between. While some of these methods try to solve imbalanced data problems by simply manipulating the distribution of the classes in the training data, others try to manipulate the learning process by adjusting the underlying algorithm or adjusting the cost of performance to favor the accurate prediction of the minority class.

![Figure 6.11 A Taxonomy of Methods Used to Handle the Imbalanced Data Problems](graphics/06fig11.jpg)

**Figure 6.11** A Taxonomy of Methods Used to Handle the Imbalanced Data Problems

Data sampling methods—the most widely used class of methods in data science and machine learning because of their ease of understanding, formulation, and implementation—either increase the number of minority examples by generating additional examples (collectively called oversampling methods) or decrease the number of majority examples by removing some of them (usually called undersampling methods).

Oversampling methods increase the number of examples in the minority class in one of two ways: (1) replicating the examples in the minority class until they are equal to the number of examples in the majority class (via a simple copy and paste or bootstrapping-type random sampling) or (2) synthetically creating new examples that are not the same but are very similar to the samples in the minority class. A popular technique called SMOTE (Synthetic Minority Oversampling Technique) synthetically creates similar minority class examples by using the *k*\-nearest neighbor algorithm to increase the number of examples in the minority class. SMOTE works quite well with data sets that are dominated by genuinely numeric input variables; on the other hand, it does a very poor job with data sets that are dominated by categorical/nominal variables. There have been numerous attempts to create variants of SMOTE algorithms to address the shortcomings of the original formulation.

Undersampling, on the other hand, keeps the minority class examples as is and randomly samples equal numbers of examples from the majority class. That is, some of the majority class examples are excluded from the training data set. The random selection can be with replacement (i.e., bootstrapping) or without replacement.

Another class of methods used to handle imbalanced data problems, called cost-sensitive methods, focuses on misclassification cost. Whereas data sampling methods focus on balancing the data prior to the learning/training process, cost-sensitive methods focus on adjustment to the cost of classification performance during the model building (i.e., training) progress. Specifically, this class of methods assigns different misclassification costs to various classes based on the severity of the imbalance in the data. In practice, these methods aim to either adjust the classification threshold or assign disproportional cost to classes to enhance the focus on the minority class.

Another type of method to handle the imbalanced data problem focuses on formulaic adjustments to a variety of classification algorithms. Although these adjusted classification algorithms all have the same goal (i.e., to mitigate the effect of imbalanced data), the specific methods the algorithms use to achieve this goal differ significantly. The support vector machine and its variants benefit most from this type of mitigation. For example, in a recent research project, Piri et al. (2018) developed a novel synthetic informative minority oversampling (SIMO) algorithm that employs a support vector machine to enhance the learning from imbalanced data sets by creating synthetic examples of the minority class near the support vectors (i.e., the hard-to-classify regions in the multidimensional feature space).

The one-class method has also been used by the machine learning community to address imbalanced data problems. The general idea behind the one-class method is to focus on only one class label at a time. That is, the training data samples are all from a single class (e.g., all positive or all negative samples) in order to learn only the specific characteristics of that class. Because this method focuses on recognizing and predicting only one class, it is often called a recognition-based method; in contrast, discrimination-based methods are commonly used in ordinary classification, where models are developed to discern/differentiate between two or more classes. Because the main idea behind the one-class method is to make the underlying model specific to the characteristics of one class and to recognize everything else as not belonging to that class, it uses three unification/homogenization approaches: (1) density-based characterization, (2) boundary determination, and (3) reconstruction/evolution-based specification. These approaches borrow their conceptual depiction and related functionalities from, and hence can be thought of as extensions of, clustering algorithms such as *k*\-means, *k*\-medoids, *k*\-centers, and self-organizing maps.

Some of the latest methods to handle imbalanced data problems emerged from the field of method ensembles. As opposed to using a single prediction model, this type of model uses a number of either the same type of methods (homogeneous ensembles) or different prediction methods (heterogeneous ensembles) to mitigate the negative effects of the imbalanced data. Variants of both bagging and boosting ensemble methods have been proposed as solutions to imbalanced data problems. In the case of bagging, the random sampling is adjusted to favor the minority class, and in the case of boosting, the weights for the minority class samples are adjusted disproportionally. Active learning—a methodology that involves learning iteratively in a piecewise manner—is also being explored as a way to handle imbalanced data problems.

Despite the fact that there have been numerous efforts to address the imbalanced data problem in the machine learning community, the current state of the art is still limited to heuristic solutions and ad hoc methodologies. There is still an absence of universally accepted theories, methodologies, and best practices. Although most studies claim to have developed data balancing methodologies that improve the prediction accuracy of the minority class, a significant number of studies have also concluded that these methodologies worsen the prediction accuracy of the majority class and the overall classification accuracy. Ongoing research is still seeking to answer questions such as “Can there be a single universal methodology that produces better prediction results?” or “Can there be a methodology that can prescribe the best data balancing algorithm for a given machine learning method and the specifics of the data on hand?”

### **Explainability of Machine Learning Models for Predictive Analytics**

In predictive analytics (and machine learning), there has been a trade-off between model complexity and model performance so that the more complex the machine learning models (e.g., deep neural networks, random forest, gradient boosting), the more accurate the prediction outcomes. As a model gets simpler (i.e., moving toward more parsimony), less predictive performance is observed, but at the same time, the model becomes more interpretable and transparent. The seminal research paper “Why Should I Trust You” by Ribeiro et al. (2016) appropriately highlights the issue with machine learning models being black boxes. With the new and enhanced interest in machine learning methods—especially since the advent of model ensembles and deep learning—model interpretability has become a fast-growing field of research; today this trend is appropriately called explainable AI and human-interpretable machine learning.

As you have seen in this book, predictive analytics methods and their underlying machine learning algorithms are really good at capturing complex relationships between input and output variables (producing very accurate prediction models), but they are not nearly as good at explaining how they do what they do (i.e., model transparency/model explainability). In order to mitigate this deficiency (commonly called “black-box syndrome”), the machine learning community has proposed several methods, most of which are characterized as sensitivity analysis. Some of these methods are global (providing an explanation based on the average score of all data samples) and some are local (providing a single sample-level explanation). In the context of predictive modeling, sensitivity analysis usually refers to an exclusive experimentation process designed and executed to discover the cause-and-effect relationship between the input and output variables. Some of the variable importance methods are model/algorithm specific (i.e., applied to decision trees, neural networks, random forest), and some are model/algorithm agnostic (i.e., applied to any and every predictive model). Here are the most commonly used variable importance methods employed in machine learning and predictive modeling:

*   **Developing and observing a well-trained decision tree model to see the relative discernibility of the input variables.** The closer to the root of the tree a variable is used to split, the greater is its importance or relative contribution to the prediction model.
    
*   **Developing and observing a rich and large random forest model and assessing the variable split statistics.** If the ratio of a given variable’s selection to candidate counts (i.e., the number of times a variable is selected as the level 0 splitter divided by the number of time it was picked randomly as one of the split candidates) is larger, then its importance/relative contribution is also greater. This process can be extended to the top three layers of the trees to generate a weighted average of the split statistics and can be used as a measure of variable importance for random forest models.
    
*   **Sensitivity analysis based on input value perturbation, where the input variables are gradually and systematically changed/perturbed one at a time and the relative change in the output is observed.** The larger the change in the output, the greater the importance of the perturbed variable. This method is often used in trained feed-forward neural network modeling where all of the input variables are numeric and standardized/normalized.
    
*   **Sensitivity analysis based on leave-one-out methodology.** This method can be applied to any type of predictive analytics method; that is, it is predictive model agnostic. Because of its general applicability and ease of implementation, this sensitivity analysis method is used in several commercial and free/open source analytics tools as the default functionality; it is explained further later in this section.
    
*   **Sensitivity analysis based on developing a surrogate model to assess the variable importance of a single record/sample using LIME and SHAP methodologies.** Whereas the methods listed previously are considered global interpreters, LIME and SHAP are called local because they explain the importance of the variables at the sample level (as opposed to the average of all samples). Because of the increased interest in the LIME and SHAP methods, they are explained later in this section.
    

#### ***Sensitivity Analysis Based on Leave-One-Out Methodology***

Sensitivity analysis based on leave-one-out methodology relies on the experimental process of systematically removing input variables, one at a time, from the input variable set, developing and testing a model, and observing the impact of the absence of this variable on the predictive performance of the machine learning model. Specifically, the model is first trained and tested (often using *k*\-fold cross-validation) with all input variables, and the best predictive accuracy is recorded as the baseline. Then, the same model with exactly the same settings is trained and tested with all but one input variable, and the best prediction accuracy is recorded. This process is repeated once for each variable, each time excluding a different input variable. Then the degradation from the baseline is measured for the omission of each variable, and these measures are used to create a tabular and graphical illustration of the variable importance, like the one shown in [Figure 6.12](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig12).

![Figure 6.12 A Graphical Depiction of the Sensitivity Analysis Process](graphics/06fig12.jpg)

**Figure 6.12** A Graphical Depiction of the Sensitivity Analysis Process

This sensitivity analysis method can be used for any predictive analytics and machine learning method. Historically, it has often been used for support vector machines, decision trees, logistic regression, and artificial neural networks. Saltelli (2002) formalized the algebraic representation of this measurement process with the following equation:

![Images](graphics/pg225-01.jpg)

In this equation, the denominator *V(F*[*t*]*)* refers to the variance in the output variable. In the numerator, *V*(*E*(*F*[*t*]|*X*[*i*])), *E* is the expectation operator to call for an integral over parameter *X*[*i*]; that is, inclusive of all input variables except *X*[*i*], the *V*, the variance operator applies a further integral over *X*[*i*]. The variable contribution (i.e., importance), represented as *S*[*i*], for the *i*th variable is calculated as the normalized sensitivity measure. In a later study, Saltelli et al. (2004) proved that this equation is the most probable measure of model sensitivity that is capable of ranking input variables (i.e., predictors) in the order of importance for any combination of interactions, including the non-orthogonal relationships among the input variables.

Using this method of sensitivity analysis may produce slightly different importance measures for different model types for the same data set. It is possible to either select and use the variable importance measured for the most predictive model type while ignoring the ones generated by the other model types or to use some combination (i.e., ensemble) of all importance measures produced by all model types. One way to properly combine the sensitivity analysis results for all prediction model types is to use an information fusion-based methodology—a weighted average in which the weights are determined based on the prediction power of the individual model types. Particularly, it is possible to modify the preceding equation in such a way that the sensitivity measure of an input variable *n* is obtained based on the information combined (i.e., fused) from *m* number of prediction model types. The following equation represents this weighted summation function:

![Images](graphics/pg225-02.jpg)

In this equation ω[*i*] represents the normalized contribution/weight for each prediction model, where the level of contribution/weight of a model is calculated as a function of its relative predictive power. The larger the prediction power (i.e., accuracy), the greater the value of ω.

#### ***Local Interpretability with LIME and SHAP***

In linear regression models, *beta* coefficients explain the prediction for all the data points; that is, if a variable value increases by one unit, the prediction value increases by beta for every data point. This is commonly called *global interpretability*, and in causal modeling, it is called *average causal analysis* (Nayak, 2019). However, global interpretability does not specifically explain individual differences; for example, the effect of variable values for rejecting the loan application of one applicant could be different from that of another. *Local interpretability* provides an explanation for an individual data point or local subsection of the joint distribution of independent variables. LIME and SHAP are two recent and popular methods that aim to provide local explainability by building surrogate models to the black boxes. They tweak the input slightly (as is done in sensitivity analysis) and test the changes in prediction through a surrogate model representation (see [Figure 6.13](#ch06.xhtml#ch06fig13)). Because these surrogate models still treat the machine learning models as black boxes, these methodologies are considered model agnostic.

![Figure 6.13 From Global Black-Box Model to Local Surrogate Model for Variable Importance](graphics/06fig13.jpg)

**Figure 6.13** From Global Black-Box Model to Local Surrogate Model for Variable Importance

Local Interpretable Model-Agnostic Explanations (LIME) is a relatively new variable assessment method developed to explain the prediction of any prediction model (i.e., classifier) in a human-interpretable manner by developing/learning a surrogate model locally based on the specifics of the prediction (i.e., the predicted case/sample). The main idea behind LIME is to simulate the relationships between inputs and outputs of a complex machine learning model by developing a surrogate model (which is similar but much simpler and explainable) for explaining the prediction results of a single record (i.e., a specific customer). To do so, the surrogate model utilizes a sample of synthetically generated records that closely resemble the actual predicted record. LIME is a popular technique because it is model agnostic (i.e., it works with any type of prediction model), it can generate human-interpretable outcomes, it provides an explanation at the local (single record) level, it is additive (i.e., the sum of importance values of all the variables for a data point is equal to the final prediction), and it is a computationally very efficient algorithm. For more on LIME and its algorithmic details, see Ribeiro et al. (2016).

Shapley Additive Explanations (SHAP) is another recently proposed technique for model interpretation. It is widely considered the best local model interpretation technique and is superior to LIME in several dimensions. Although SHAP employs a similar concept to LIME, it provides theoretical guarantees based on the game theory concept of Shapley values; it is capable of capturing and representing complex relationships at the local level beyond just linear relationships, and produces more accurate, robust, and reliable interpretation outcomes. SHAP can also be used effectively for variable selection: You can select a subset of variables with higher variable importance and drop other variables if you want to reduce the number of variables. Because of its consistency, the order of variable importance does not change, so less important variables can be ignored. For more details on SHAP, see Lundberg and Lee (2017).

#### ***Which Technique Is the Best?***

As is the case almost always, there is no magic answer or solution to this question that applies to all problems and data sets. If you are interested in model-agnostic global explainability, perhaps leave-one-out sensitivity analysis could be used. If a model-specific interpretation is desired, then the appropriate sensitivity analysis for the specific model type could be used. If you desire local interpretation, then either LIME of SHAP can be used; of these two, SHAP is considered a better method, but it is computationally more expensive than LIME. If a quick explanation at the single record level is needed for a large and complex problem, perhaps LIME might be the method of choice.

### **Summary**

Predictive analytics is a developing area that involves novel methods and methodologies. The idea behind these developments is to address the shortcomings of the existing techniques in order to create more accurate and more explainable models. This chapter covers several recent advances in predictive analytics: model ensembles, bias–variance trade-off, imbalanced data problems, and explainable AI/machine learning. While the first three of these advances focus on building more accurate and unbiased predictive models, explainable AI/machine learning focuses on making the black-box models more transparent so that they can be used in domains where explainability is one of the required characteristics in a prediction model.
